<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct a smooth projective complex fourfold with large fundamental group whose universal cover is not Stein. The universal cover contains no positive-dimensional compact complex-analytic subvariety, so this disproves Shafarevich's holomorphic-convexity conjecture even under the large-fundamental-group hypothesis.

- **claim-main-fourfold** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There exist a smooth connected projective complex fourfold $X$ and a simple abelian surface $A\subset X$ such that* 1. *$X$ has large fundamental group;* 2. *$\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(\pi_1(A)\to\pi_1(X)\bigr)\simeq\mathbb Z$.* *The universal cover $\widetilde X$ contains no positive-dimensional compact complex analytic subvariety and is not Stein.
  - Explanation: A smooth projective fourfold with large fundamental group exists whose universal cover has no compact positive-dimensional analytic subvariety and is not Stein. It contains a simple abelian surface whose fundamental group has infinite cyclic image.
- **claim-large-reduction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-large-reduction)): Let $A$ be a simple abelian surface and let $A\hookrightarrow S'$ be a closed embedding in a smooth connected projective complex variety. Suppose that $$\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(\pi_1(A)\longrightarrow\pi_1(S')\bigr)\simeq\mathbb Z.$$ There exist a smooth connected projective fourfold $X$ with large fundamental group and a closed embedding $A\hookrightarrow X$ with infinite cyclic image on fundamental groups, such that the universal cover of $X$ is not Stein.
  - Explanation: An embedding of a simple abelian surface into a smooth projective variety with infinite cyclic fundamental-group image yields a fourfold with large fundamental group and non-Stein universal cover, via a general complete intersection in a product with an abelian variety.
- **claim-compact-criterion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-red-compact-criterion)): Let $X$ be a smooth connected projective complex variety. Its universal cover contains no positive-dimensional compact complex-analytic subvariety if and only if $X$ has large fundamental group.
  - Explanation: Absence of positive-dimensional compact analytic subvarieties in the universal cover is equivalent to largeness of the fundamental group, proved with the proper mapping theorem and Chow's theorem.
- **claim-interpolation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-red-interpolation)): Let $W$ be a projective variety, $F\subset W$ a closed subscheme, and $L$ a very ample line bundle. Fix a positive integer $\ell$. For all sufficiently large $m$ and every set of distinct points $p_1,\ldots,p_\ell\in W\setminus F$, the evaluation map $$H^0(W,\mathcal I_F\otimes L^m) \longrightarrow \bigoplus_{i=1}^{\ell}L^m\|_{p_i}$$ is surjective. The same bound on $m$ works for every such set.
  - Explanation: For sufficiently large twist, sections of the twisted ideal sheaf surject onto the fibers at any finite set of points outside the subscheme, with one bound valid for every such set.
- **claim-path-criterion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-path-lemma)): There is a bound $N_0$, depending only on the common hyperbolicity constant, with the following property. If the readings satisfy <span class="nodecor">(P1)–(P3)</span> with $N\ge N_0$, and $e$ is a single-edge loop at $v$ satisfying $[e]_v\ne d_v$, then $e$ is nontrivial in $\mathcal Q$.
  - Explanation: Under uniformly hyperbolic chart readings with a long enough reading range, a single-edge loop whose read endpoint differs from the base state stays nontrivial in the quotient groupoid.
- **claim-path-narrowing** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-narrowing)): If two guides have a ladder of width at most $1000H$, then they have one of width at most $H$.
  - Explanation: A moderately wide ladder between two guides can be replaced by a ladder of the basic width, using slimness of quadrangles in a single chart.
- **claim-path-extension** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-extension)): If $\alpha$ is a guide and $e$ is an edge starting at its terminal vertex, there is a guide $\beta$ to the endpoint of $\alpha e$ and a ladder between $\alpha e$ and $\beta$ of width at most $50H$.
  - Explanation: Appending an edge to a guide can be repaired to a guide ending at the same vertex, joined to the extended path by a ladder of bounded width, by minimizing distances between chosen nearby vertices.
- **claim-arrangement-local** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-arrangement-local)): Given $W>0$ and $R_0>0$, there is a torsion-free principal congruence subgroup $\Gamma_0$, normalized by $\sigma$, with the following properties. Let $\mathcal A_0$ be the union of all $\Gamma_0$-translates of the labeled hyperplanes of $\mathcal D$.* 1. *The arrangement $\mathcal A_0$ is locally finite. The hyperplanes meeting any ball of radius $W$ have distinct labels and are carried to the corresponding subarrangement of $\mathcal D$ by a holomorphic isometry induced by a unitary map sending each prescribed translated normal to its prototype normal.* 2. *The injectivity radius of $\Gamma_0\backslash\mathbb B$ is greater than $R_0$.* 3. *In the ball of radius $R_0$ about $o$, the arrangement is exactly $\mathcal D$ restricted to that ball. The analogous assertion holds in every $\Gamma_0$-translate of this ball.* *All these conclusions concerning the lifted arrangement remain valid on any subsequent finite cover of $\Gamma_0\backslash\mathbb B$, provided one uses the full inverse image of the arrangement.
  - Explanation: A sufficiently deep principal congruence subgroup reproduces the prototype labeled hyperplane arrangement exactly in bounded balls and has large injectivity radius; the properties persist on finite covers with the full inverse image arrangement.
- **claim-stover-toledo** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geo-stover-toledo)): Let $M_0$ be a compact ball quotient of complex dimension at least two by a torsion-free congruence arithmetic lattice of simplest type. Let $D$ be a nonempty totally geodesic divisor whose components are smooth, embedded, and pairwise disjoint. For every integer $d\ge2$ there is a finite unramified cover $M_1\to M_0$ such that $M_1$ admits a cyclic cover of degree $d$, branched over the full inverse image of $D$.
  - Explanation: Virtual cyclic ramified covers: a compact congruence ball quotient has a finite unramified cover admitting a cyclic cover of any degree at least two branched over a disjoint smooth totally geodesic divisor.
- **claim-orbifold-surface** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-orbifold-surface)): After any prescribed finite lower bounds on the arrangement radii and injectivity radius, the construction above gives a smooth connected proper effective orbifold $\mathcal S$ with projective coarse space, a simple abelian surface $A=\mathop{\mathrm{Jac}}(C)$, an iterated point blowup $Y\to A$, and a morphism $f:Y\to\mathcal S$ with image in its ordinary locus. The distinguished fiber is obtained from the product $C\times C$, its normal bundle $\operatorname{pr}_1^*L\oplus\operatorname{pr}_2^*L$, and the action <a href="#geo:line-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:line-action">[geo:line-action]</a> by the explicit blowups and coarse quotients above. Away from that fiber the orbifold is $[V/\langle s\rangle]$, with $V$ given by the two parity covers and the filling rules of Section <a href="#geo:fill" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:fill">4.4</a>.
  - Explanation: The explicit blowups, sign covers and coarse quotients give a smooth proper orbifold with projective coarse space and a map from a point blowup of the Jacobian surface into its ordinary locus.
- **claim-label-extension** (theorem, [source](#stmt-mod-extension)): The homomorphisms $\ell_z$ and $\ell_w$ extend across the blowups and branched fillings of the finite model prescribed in Section <a href="#geo:fill" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:fill">4.4</a>.
  - Explanation: The translation homomorphisms kill every meridian of the exceptional or filled divisors, so they extend over the filled finite model.
- **claim-central-descent** (theorem, [source](#stmt-mod-central)): The regular covering associated with $\ker\ell$ admits an order-four lift of $s$ commuting with its deck translations. Its quotient by this lift is an ordinary topological covering of the coarse space $\overline E/\langle s\rangle$. Consequently it pulls back to a covering of the resolution used over the distinguished fiber.
  - Explanation: The sum cover carries an order-four lift of the symmetry commuting with deck translations, so its quotient is an ordinary covering of the coarse quotient and pulls back to the resolution.
- **claim-buffer-bound** (theorem, [source](#stmt-mod-buffer-bound)): For every $R>0$ there is $B_1(R)$ with the following property. In any finite model with a complete $z$ pencil, if a loop in the sign cover projects into $B(x,R)$ and has nonzero $z$ translation, then $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,L_z)\le B_1(R).$$ The analogous assertion holds for $w$. The same bounds hold if the loops must avoid any additional analytic subsets.
  - Explanation: A loop supporting nonzero translation in a ball of fixed radius forces the ball center to lie uniformly close to the pencil center.
- **claim-kernel-compatibility** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-kernel-compatibility)): For every $R>0$ there are constants $B(R)>R$ and $J_0(R)$, depending only on $\mathcal D$, with the following properties.* 1. *Suppose two finite-model descriptions, with their prescribed normal matches, agree on all hyperplanes meeting $B(x,B(R))$. Identify their sign covers there by a fixed choice of sheets. On loops in this sign cover that project into $B(x,R)$, their individual $z$ kernels agree and their individual $w$ kernels agree. More precisely, for each family the corresponding integer homomorphisms agree up to sign. The descriptions may include additional hyperplanes outside the buffered ball.* 2. *In the full model, if $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,o)>J_0(R)$, then on such loops $$\ker(\ell_z+\ell_w)=\ker\ell_z\cap\ker\ell_w.$$* *Both statements remain valid after restricting the loops to avoid any further analytic subsets. The first statement is equivariant under the symmetry interchanging the families.
  - Explanation: Individual translation kernels agree on loops in a ball whenever the two model descriptions agree on a buffered ball, and the sum kernel equals the intersection of the individual kernels far from the distinguished point.
- **claim-model-hyperbolicity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-model-hyperbolicity)): There are $u>0$ and $\delta\ge0$, depending only on $\mathcal D$, such that every connected component of every $\mathcal G_T(u)$ and of $\mathcal G_{\mathrm{cen}}(u)$ is $\delta$-hyperbolic. The same $\delta$ can be used if an arbitrary locally finite union of proper complex analytic subsets of $\mathbb B$ is additionally omitted, with invariant omissions in the central case. On the vertices retained after such an omission, graph distances are unchanged.
  - Explanation: The path graphs defined by projected diameter are uniformly Gromov hyperbolic, also after omitting locally finite unions of proper analytic subsets, which leaves distances unchanged.
- **claim-bounded-core** (theorem, [source](#stmt-mod-bounded-core)): One can choose $u$, simultaneously for all the finite models, so that the vertices projecting into any fixed bounded radial region have bounded graph diameter within each connected component.
  - Explanation: Deck translations over bounded regions can be traversed by single edges, so vertices over a bounded radial region have bounded graph diameter.
- **claim-cone-hyperbolic** (theorem, [source](#stmt-mod-cone)): The graph $\mathcal C(I)$ is hyperbolic with an absolute constant, independent of the bounded metric space $I$. More precisely, put $$j=\min\{t,t',-\log d_I(\xi',\eta')\}, \qquad -\log0=+\infty.$$ Its distance $D$ satisfies $$\label{mod:cone-distance} t+t'-2j\le D\bigl((\xi',t),(\eta',t')\bigr) \le t+t'-2j+3.$$
  - Explanation: The discrete cone graph over any bounded metric space is hyperbolic with an absolute constant, with explicit two-sided distance estimates in terms of heights and the separation of the base points.
- **claim-orbifold-cyclic** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-orbifold-cyclic)): For a sufficiently deep choice of the arithmetic level in the construction of Proposition <a href="#prop:orbifold-surface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:orbifold-surface">10</a>, the map $f:Y\longrightarrow\mathcal S$ satisfies $$\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(f_*:\pi_1(Y)\longrightarrow\pi_1(\mathcal S)\bigr)\cong\mathbb Z.$$ Here $Y$ is an iterated point blowup of the simple abelian surface $\mathop{\mathrm{Jac}}(C)$, and its image lies in the ordinary locus of $\mathcal S$.
  - Explanation: For a sufficiently deep arithmetic level the image of the fundamental group of the blown-up surface in that of the orbifold is infinite cyclic.
- **claim-surface-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-app-surface-comparison)): With common choices of basing, the images in $\pi_1(\mathcal S)$ of the preceding generators of $\pi_1(Y)$ equal the images, under <a href="#app:comparison-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="app:comparison-map">[app:comparison-map]</a>, of the corresponding loops in $C\times\{q_0\}\subset V$. The loops can also be represented in the sign-covered arrangement complement, arbitrarily close to $F_*$, with both normal coordinates nonzero.
  - Explanation: Generators of the surface group map to the same elements as the corresponding loops near the distinguished fiber, and these loops can be pushed into the sign-covered arrangement complement.
- **claim-cyclic-upper** (theorem, [source](#stmt-app-cyclic-upper)): The image of $\pi_1(C\times\{q_0\})$ in $\pi_1(V)$ is cyclic. Consequently the image of $\pi_1(Y)$ in $\pi_1(\mathcal S)$ is cyclic.
  - Explanation: Coupling relations identify the four red meridians and then the two blue ones, so the image of the surface group is cyclic.
- **claim-chart-axioms** (theorem, [source](#stmt-app-chart-axioms)): The readings just constructed satisfy the three chart hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#thm:path-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:path-lemma">5</a>.
  - Explanation: The central and individual chart readings satisfy the continuation, prefix-comparison and hyperbolicity hypotheses of the local path criterion.
- **claim-orbifold-meridians** (theorem, [source](#stmt-app-orbifold-meridians)): Let $\mathcal T$ be a connected smooth effective complex orbifold, and let $U$ be a dense ordinary open subset whose complement is complex analytic. Then $\pi_1(U)\to\pi_1(\mathcal T)$ is surjective. Its kernel is normally generated by the loops obtained from boundaries of small transverse disks in smooth orbifold charts at general points of the omitted divisors. Equivalently, if the generic stabilizer along such a divisor has order $m$, one kills the $m$th power of a small meridian in the ordinary quotient. Omitted strata of complex codimension at least two add no relations.
  - Explanation: The orbifold fundamental group is the quotient of that of the ordinary open set by the powers of transverse meridians over the omitted divisors.
- **claim-true-relations** (theorem, [source](#stmt-app-true-relations)): A loop of edges of $G$ that is nullhomotopic in $\mathcal S$ is trivial in $\mathcal P(G)$.
  - Explanation: A loop of graph edges that is nullhomotopic in the orbifold is trivial in the path groupoid, by subdividing homotopies and checking transverse-disk relations in charts.
- **claim-ambient-realization** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-ambient-realization)): Let $\mathcal T$ be a smooth connected effective proper complex Deligne–Mumford stack with projective coarse space. Let $Y$ be a smooth projective surface and let $f:Y\to\mathcal T$ have image in the locus with trivial stabilizers. There exist a smooth connected projective variety $S$, an embedding $i:Y\hookrightarrow S$, and a morphism $h:S\to\mathcal T$ such that $h\circ i=f$ and $$h_*:\pi_1(S)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\pi_1(\mathcal T).$$ One may take $\dim S=5$.
  - Explanation: A surface mapping into the ordinary locus of a proper stack embeds in a smooth projective variety of the stated dimension with the same fundamental group, via a projective bundle and general hyperplane sections through the surface.
- **claim-ambient-blowdown** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-ambient-blowdown)): Let $Y_1\hookrightarrow S$ be an embedding of a smooth projective surface in a smooth connected projective variety, and let $b:Y_1\to Y_0$ be the blowdown of a $(-1)$-curve to a smooth projective surface. There exist a smooth connected projective variety $S_0$, an embedding $Y_0\hookrightarrow S_0$, and an isomorphism $\theta:\pi_1(S)\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_1(S_0)$ such that $$\theta\circ(\pi_1(Y_1)\to\pi_1(S)) = (\pi_1(Y_0)\to\pi_1(S_0))\circ b_*.$$ The equality is understood with compatible basepoints, or up to conjugation.
  - Explanation: A point blowdown of an embedded surface can be realized in a new smooth projective ambient variety, preserving the ambient fundamental group and the induced surface homomorphism.
- **claim-cyclic-embedding** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-cyclic-embedding)): There exist a simple abelian surface $A$, a smooth connected projective complex variety $S'$, and a closed embedding $A\hookrightarrow S'$ such that $$\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(\pi_1(A)\longrightarrow\pi_1(S')\bigr)\simeq\mathbb Z.$$
  - Explanation: A simple abelian surface embeds in a smooth projective variety with infinite cyclic fundamental-group image, obtained by removing the surface blowups one at a time.

```json
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-orbifold-surface",
        "exact_quote": "After any prescribed finite lower bounds on the arrangement radii and injectivity radius, the construction above gives a smooth connected proper effective orbifold $\\mathcal S$ with projective coarse space, a simple abelian surface $A=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Jac}}(C)$, an iterated point blowup $Y\\to A$, and a morphism $f:Y\\to\\mathcal S$ with image in its ordinary locus. The distinguished fiber is obtained from the product $C\\times C$, its normal bundle $\\operatorname{pr}_1^*L\\oplus\\operatorname{pr}_2^*L$, and the action <a href=\"#geo:line-action\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geo:line-action\">[geo:line-action]</a> by the explicit blowups and coarse quotients above. Away from that fiber the orbifold is $[V/\\langle s\\rangle]$, with $V$ given by the two parity covers and the filling rules of Section <a href=\"#geo:fill\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geo:fill\">4.4</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-046.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

Away from that fiber the orbifold is $[V/\\langle s\\rangle]$, with $V$ given by the two parity covers and the filling rules of Section <a href=\"#geo:fill\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geo:fill\">4.4</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-046.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-kernel-compatibility",
        "exact_quote": "For every $R>0$ there are constants $B(R)>R$ and $J_0(R)$, depending only on $\\mathcal D$, with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Suppose two finite-model descriptions, with their prescribed normal matches, agree on all hyperplanes meeting $B(x,B(R))$. Identify their sign covers there by a fixed choice of sheets. On loops in this sign cover that project into $B(x,R)$, their individual $z$ kernels agree and their individual $w$ kernels agree. More precisely, for each family the corresponding integer homomorphisms agree up to sign. The descriptions may include additional hyperplanes outside the buffered ball.*\n\n2.  *In the full model, if $\\mathop{\\mathrm{dist}}(x,o)>J_0(R)$, then on such loops $$\\ker(\\ell_z+\\ell_w)=\\ker\\ell_z\\cap\\ker\\ell_w.$$*\n\n*Both statements remain valid after restricting the loops to avoid any further analytic subsets. The first statement is equivariant under the symmetry interchanging the families.",
        "source_id": "PM-046.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-kernel-compatibility",
          "exact_quote": "For every $R>0$ there are constants $B(R)>R$ and $J_0(R)$, depending only on $\\mathcal D$, with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Suppose two finite-model descriptions, with their prescribed normal matches, agree on all hyperplanes meeting $B(x,B(R))$. Identify their sign covers there by a fixed choice of sheets. On loops in this sign cover that project into $B(x,R)$, their individual $z$ kernels agree and their individual $w$ kernels agree. More precisely, for each family the corresponding integer homomorphisms agree up to sign. The descriptions may include additional hyperplanes outside the buffered ball.*\n\n2.  *In the full model, if $\\mathop{\\mathrm{dist}}(x,o)>J_0(R)$, then on such loops $$\\ker(\\ell_z+\\ell_w)=\\ker\\ell_z\\cap\\ker\\ell_w.$$*\n\n*Both statements remain valid after restricting the loops to avoid any further analytic subsets. The first statement is equivariant under the symmetry interchanging the families.",
          "source_id": "PM-046.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "For every $R>0$ there are constants $B(R)>R$ and $J_0(R)$, depending only on $\\mathcal D$, with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Suppose two finite-model descriptions, with their prescribed normal matches, agree on all hyperplanes meeting $B(x,B(R))$.         "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal T$ be a connected smooth effective complex orbifold, and let $U$ be a dense ordinary open subset whose complement is complex analytic. Then $\\pi_1(U)\\to\\pi_1(\\mathcal T)$ is surjective. Its kernel is normally generated by the loops obtained from boundaries of small transverse disks in smooth orbifold charts at general points of the omitted divisors. Equivalently, if the generic stabilizer along such a divisor has order $m$, one kills the $m$th power of a small meridian in the ordinary quotient. Omitted strata of complex codimension at least two add no relations.",
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **large fundamental group** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Recall that $X$ has *large fundamental group* if, for every positive-dimensional closed irreducible subvariety $Z\subset X$, the image of $\pi_1(Z^\nu)\to\pi_1(X)$ is infinite; here $Z^\nu$ is the normalization.
- **ordinary point** ([source](#pm-h-6)): An *ordinary* point has trivial stabilizer.
- **closes on reading** ([source](#pm-h-9)): A loop $p$ at $v$ *closes on reading* if $[p]_v=d_v$.
- **guide** ([source](#pm-h-10)): A *guide* is a path each of whose subpaths of length at most $k$ reads as a geodesic in the chart at its initial vertex.
- **ladder of width h** ([source](#pm-h-11)): For paths $\alpha,\beta$ with the same initial and terminal vertices, a *ladder of width $h$* consists of the following data.
- **red and blue slopes** ([source](#geo:slopes)): We call the first four slopes *red* and the last two *blue*.
- **filled finite model** ([source](#pm-h-22)): The resulting smooth space is its *filled finite model*.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-compact-criterion** [claim](#stmt-red-compact-criterion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-compact-criterion): Proper mapping theorem and Chow's theorem in both directions; finite maps have finite-index image on fundamental groups. -> Equivalence of no compact analytic subvarieties in the universal cover with largeness
- **step-interpolation** [claim](#stmt-red-interpolation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-interpolation): Build sections nonzero at one point and vanishing at the others from global generation and very ampleness, giving a diagonal basis. -> Surjective evaluation maps for all large twists
- **step-large-reduction** [claim](#stmt-prop-large-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-interpolation-2): General complete intersection in a product with an abelian variety, Bertini and Lefschetz for smoothness and fundamental group, incidence count for fibers, Andreotti–Frankel for non-Steinness. -> Fourfold with large fundamental group and non-Stein universal cover
- **step-narrowing** [claim](#stmt-path-narrowing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-narrowing): Mark the first guide and replace rungs by mates found through quadrangle slimness in a single chart. -> Ladder of basic width
- **step-extension** [claim](#stmt-path-extension) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-extension): Minimize total distance between chosen nearby vertices at marks; show block-wise geodesicity by Gromov product estimates. -> Repaired guide with a ladder to the extended path
- **step-path-criterion** [claim](#stmt-thm-path-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-extension-2): Define a well-defined class of guides modulo narrow ladders, invariant under the relations; a trivial edge would force read endpoint equality. -> Nontriviality of the single-edge loop
- **step-arrangement-local** [claim](#stmt-prop-arrangement-local) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-arrangement-local): Bound Gram entries in a ball, separate them by a deep congruence level, extend equality of Gram matrices to isometries. -> Exact local arrangements and injectivity radius
- **step-stover-toledo** [claim](#stmt-geo-stover-toledo) / [proof](#geo:signs): Quoted theorem applied separately to each label divisor to produce sign characters. -> Finite cover admitting a branched cyclic cover
- **step-orbifold-surface** [claim](#stmt-prop-orbifold-surface) / [proof](#geo:Ymap): Blow up intersection centers, normalize sign covers, resolve the fiber under the order-four lift, and map a Jacobian blowup via an equivariant rational section. -> Orbifold and map from a point blowup of the abelian surface
- **step-label-extension** [claim](#stmt-mod-extension) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mod-extension): Check that meridians of added divisors have trivial labels. -> Extension of translation homomorphisms
- **step-central-descent** [claim](#stmt-mod-central) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mod-central): Lift the symmetry fixing a chosen point and show stabilizers act trivially on fibers of the cover. -> Ordinary covering of the coarse quotient
- **step-buffer-bound** [claim](#stmt-mod-buffer-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mod-buffer-bound): Case analysis on the number of hyperplanes meeting the ball and a boundary compactness argument. -> Uniform distance bound to the pencil center
- **step-kernel-compatibility** [claim](#stmt-prop-kernel-compatibility) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-kernel-compatibility): Pass through the buffered intermediate description; bounded intersection of the two neighborhoods of pencil centers. -> Agreement of individual kernels and sum kernel
- **step-bounded-core** [claim](#stmt-mod-bounded-core) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mod-bounded-core): Choose the edge diameter above the size of finitely many generator loops and connecting paths. -> Bounded graph diameter over bounded regions
- **step-cone-hyperbolic** [claim](#stmt-mod-cone) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mod-cone): Upper bound by descending, one horizontal edge and ascending; lower bound by counting vertical edges; Gromov product criterion. -> Hyperbolicity with an absolute constant
- **step-model-hyperbolicity** [claim](#stmt-prop-model-hyperbolicity) / [proof](#pm-h-28): Quasi-isometry with the cone graph through collar coordinates and rounding, equivariance for the quotient, perturbation for omissions. -> Uniform hyperbolicity of model graphs
- **step-surface-comparison** [claim](#stmt-app-surface-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-app-surface-comparison): Lift a bouquet through the rational section and push it off the exceptional divisor by a small normal section. -> Loops in the arrangement complement near the fiber
- **step-cyclic-upper** [claim](#stmt-app-cyclic-upper) / [proof](#proof-stmt-app-cyclic-upper): Slide the red meridians to a coupling hyperplane and use the killed exceptional meridian to identify them; the sphere relation identifies the blue ones. -> Cyclic image of the surface group
- **step-chart-axioms** [claim](#stmt-app-chart-axioms) / [proof](#proof-stmt-app-chart-axioms): Verify continuation and prefix comparison for individual, central and mixed charts using the kernel compatibility. -> Chart hypotheses of the path criterion
- **step-orbifold-meridians** [claim](#stmt-app-orbifold-meridians) / [proof](#proof-stmt-app-orbifold-meridians): Frame-space presentation and general position: nullhomotopy disks meet divisors in meridians. -> Presentation of the orbifold fundamental group
- **step-true-relations** [claim](#stmt-app-true-relations) / [proof](#proof-stmt-app-true-relations): Subdivide homotopies into small triangles and check transverse-disk boundaries close in charts. -> Orbifold nullhomotopic loops are trivial in the path groupoid
- **step-orbifold-cyclic** [claim](#stmt-thm-orbifold-cyclic) / [proof](#pm-h-34): A pushed loop has every power as a single edge with distinct read endpoints, so all powers are nontrivial; combine with cyclicity. -> Infinite cyclic image in the orbifold fundamental group
- **step-ambient-realization** [claim](#stmt-lem-ambient-realization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-ambient-realization): Projective bundle with highly non-ordinary locus, general hyperplane sections through the surface, quasi-projective Lefschetz. -> Embedding in a smooth projective variety with the same fundamental group
- **step-ambient-blowdown** [claim](#stmt-lem-ambient-blowdown) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-ambient-blowdown): Embed in a threefold with nodes resolved, adjust the normal bundle, blow up the curve, build an ample bundle by Nakai–Moishezon and contract the other ruling. -> Ambient variety containing the blown-down surface
- **step-cyclic-embedding** [claim](#stmt-thm-cyclic-embedding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-cyclic-embedding): Embed the blown-up surface via the realization lemma, then apply the blowdown lemma in reverse order. -> Embedding of the simple abelian surface with infinite cyclic image
- **step-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-cyclic-embedding-2): Apply the reduction proposition to the embedding and use the compact-criterion lemma. -> Fourfold with large fundamental group and non-Stein universal cover

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-main-nonstein**: The constructed fourfold has large fundamental group, contains a simple abelian surface with infinite cyclic image on fundamental groups, and its universal cover contains no positive-dimensional compact analytic subvariety and is not Stein.
- **inv-largeness-criterion**: For a smooth connected projective complex variety, absence of positive-dimensional compact analytic subvarieties in the universal cover is equivalent to largeness of the fundamental group.
- **inv-cyclic-embedding**: A simple abelian surface embeds in a smooth connected projective variety so that its fundamental group has infinite cyclic image.
- **inv-local-path-criterion**: Under bounded-range readings in uniformly hyperbolic charts with large enough reading range, a single-edge loop whose read endpoint differs from the base state is nontrivial in the quotient path groupoid.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-no-large-group**: Apply the non-Stein obstruction to a variety without checking that its fundamental group is large. -> Without largeness, the universal cover may contain compact positive-dimensional analytic subvarieties, so the stated conclusion about the universal cover does not follow.
- **anti-effective-level**: Read off an explicit arithmetic level or an executable procedure from the construction. -> The cyclic-image theorem only asserts the conclusion for a sufficiently deep level, so no explicit level or algorithm is supplied.
- **anti-path-criterion-range**: Apply the local path criterion with an arbitrary reading range. -> The criterion needs a reading range beyond a threshold set by the hyperbolicity constant; short ranges give no conclusion.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The results are existence theorems in complex and arithmetic geometry (a fourfold, an embedding, an arithmetic level chosen sufficiently deep) with no effective bounds. The path-lemma steps work on arbitrary, possibly infinite graphs and rely on minimisation over unbounded choices, so no finite computable procedure with checkable inputs and outputs is given.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper is an existence proof for a complex fourfold. It specifies no program, library or data interface, and the arithmetic level it needs is only said to be sufficiently deep. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The geometry lives in complex hyperbolic four-space and in orbifold covers. It gives no discrete dynamics, state update or physical model that a simulation could step through. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No instrument, checker or workflow for users is developed. The text fixes working conventions for varieties and fundamental groups but nothing that a practitioner would run. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The local path criterion is proved for abstract hyperbolic graphs, whose vertices are all points of a covering space, with unbounded edge length and no local finiteness. It has no finite input, no stated termination and no complexity statement, so it is not an algorithm. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Graphs, ladders and guides are proof devices defined on infinite covering spaces with multiple edges allowed. They are not stored or indexed structures with operations and costs. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns arithmetic kernels, parallel execution or memory layout. The only numerical datum about the ambient variety is its dimension, in the embedding lemma. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Number-theoretic ingredients appear only to obtain a genus-two curve with simple Jacobian and an arithmetic lattice. No hardness assumption, protocol or security claim is made. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no data, model, loss or training procedure. The only classification-like statement is that a universal cover is not Stein, a purely geometric property. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A minimisation of a sum of distances appears inside one proof, over integer-valued choices, only to prove that a minimiser exists. It is not an optimisation problem with a solver, bound or guarantee. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The charts and readings are compatibility conditions between local covers of one geometric space. They involve no nodes, messages, failures or consensus. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contributes to complex algebraic geometry and geometric group theory, as a counterexample to a conjecture. Any use is conceptual, as a benchmark for what holomorphic convexity can and cannot imply, and no construction transfers to engineering. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-large-group-needed** (hypothesis): The non-Stein conclusion for the universal cover is tied to the large fundamental group hypothesis. It is established only for the constructed fourfold, not for arbitrary projective varieties. Blocked use: ['Declaring a given projective variety to have a non-Stein universal cover without verifying largeness']
- **limit-dimension-four** (edge_case): The main theorem is an existence statement for a fourfold containing a simple abelian surface. It does not decide anything for lower dimensions. Blocked use: ['Claiming counterexamples to holomorphic convexity in complex dimension two or three from this construction']
- **limit-simple-surface-cyclic-image** (hypothesis): The reduction proposition requires a simple abelian surface and an embedding whose fundamental-group image is infinite cyclic. Blocked use: ['Applying the reduction to a nonsimple abelian surface', 'Applying the reduction when the image is finite or noncyclic']
- **limit-deep-arithmetic-level** (nonconstructive): The infinite cyclic image is proved only for a sufficiently deep arithmetic level, with no explicit bound stated. Blocked use: ['Extracting an explicit level, algorithm or running time from the construction']
- **limit-reading-range** (hypothesis): The local path criterion needs readings satisfying the stated properties and a reading range at least a bound depending only on the common hyperbolicity constant. Blocked use: ['Using the criterion with a short reading range', 'Using it for charts that are not uniformly hyperbolic']
- **limit-ladder-width** (edge_case): Narrowing applies only to guides whose ladder width is at most the stated multiple of the narrow constant, and extension gives a bounded but larger width. Blocked use: ['Narrowing a ladder of arbitrary width', 'Assuming extension keeps the narrow width']
- **limit-stover-toledo-disjoint** (hypothesis): The cyclic ramified cover theorem needs a compact congruence ball quotient and a divisor with smooth, embedded, pairwise disjoint components. For this reason it is applied to each label divisor separately and not to the union. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to a union of intersecting divisors']
- **limit-ordinary-locus** (hypothesis): The ambient realization requires the surface map to land in the locus with trivial stabilizers of a proper stack with projective coarse space. Blocked use: ['Embedding a surface meeting nontrivial stabilizers by this lemma']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct a smooth projective complex fourfold with large fundamental group whose universal cover is not Stein. The universal cover contains no positive-dimensional compact complex-analytic subvariety, so this disproves Shafarevich's holomorphic-convexity conjecture even under the large-fundamental-group hypothesis.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $X$ be a smooth connected projective complex variety, and let $\widetilde X$ denote its simply connected topological universal cover, equipped with the lifted complex structure. Shafarevich’s holomorphic-convexity conjecture asks whether $\widetilde X$ is holomorphically convex. Its particularly direct form for varieties with large fundamental group asks whether $\widetilde X$ is Stein when it contains no positive-dimensional compact complex analytic subvariety.

Recall that $X$ has *large fundamental group* if, for every positive-dimensional closed irreducible subvariety $Z\subset X$, the image of $\pi_1(Z^\nu)\to\pi_1(X)$ is infinite; here $Z^\nu$ is the normalization. This is equivalent to the absence of positive-dimensional compact analytic subvarieties in $\widetilde X$. We prove the following.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exist a smooth connected projective complex fourfold $X$ and a simple abelian surface $A\subset X$ such that*

1.  *$X$ has large fundamental group;*

2.  *$\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(\pi_1(A)\to\pi_1(X)\bigr)\simeq\mathbb Z$.*

*The universal cover $\widetilde X$ contains no positive-dimensional compact complex analytic subvariety and is not Stein.*

</div>

The obstruction to Steinness is topological and occurs in a closed complex surface inside $\widetilde X$. Since $\pi_1(A)\simeq\mathbb Z^4$, the kernel of the homomorphism in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> has rank three. The corresponding cover of $A$ is therefore diffeomorphic to $\mathbb R\times(S^1)^3$ and has nonzero third homology. A Stein surface has the homotopy type of a CW complex of real dimension at most two. This contradiction rules out Steinness of the lifted surface and hence of $\widetilde X$. Simplicity of $A$ ensures that this topological obstruction introduces no compact complex curve in that covering.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Historical context

Shafarevich’s question extends the uniformization viewpoint for curves to universal covers of projective varieties. Kollár’s account (Kollár 1995, Introduction, Section 0.3) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Kollar1995](#ref-Kollar1995)] explains both the holomorphic-convexity conjecture and its formulation for large fundamental groups. These formulations are related by Remmert reduction: a holomorphically convex complex manifold admits a proper holomorphic map to a Stein space with connected compact analytic fibers (Eyssidieux et al. 2012, Introduction, p. 1546) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[EKPR2012](#ref-EKPR2012)]. If there are no positive-dimensional compact analytic subvarieties, all those fibers are points and the reduction is an isomorphism. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> therefore also disproves the holomorphic-convexity conjecture.

An important development was the construction of almost-holomorphic reduction maps by Campana and Kollár (Campana 1994; Kollár 1993, 1995) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Campana1994](#ref-Campana1994), [Kollar1993](#ref-Kollar1993), [Kollar1995](#ref-Kollar1995)]. They describe, through a very general point, the normalized subvarieties whose fundamental groups have finite image in the ambient group. Their existence provides an algebraic and geometric description of the part of the variety that such subvarieties fill. Holomorphic convexity asks in addition for a proper holomorphic reduction of the universal cover to a Stein space. The large case makes this additional analytic requirement especially visible: the expected reduction has no positive-dimensional fibers.

Linearity of the fundamental group supports strong affirmative results. Katzarkov and Ramachandran proved holomorphic convexity for projective surfaces whose fundamental group admits a faithful complex representation with reductive Zariski closure (Katzarkov and Ramachandran 1998) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[KatzarkovRamachandran1998](#ref-KatzarkovRamachandran1998)]. Eyssidieux treated the reductive linear case in arbitrary dimension (Eyssidieux 2004) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Eyssidieux2004](#ref-Eyssidieux2004)]. Eyssidieux, Katzarkov, Pantev, and Ramachandran proved the linear Shafarevich conjecture for smooth projective varieties (Eyssidieux et al. 2012) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[EKPR2012](#ref-EKPR2012)]. More recently, Bakker, Brunebarbe, and Tsimerman developed linear Shafarevich theory for normal algebraic spaces and quasiprojective varieties (Bakker, Brunebarbe, and Tsimerman 2024) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[BBT2024](#ref-BBT2024)]. The construction here concerns the unrestricted fundamental group. Its infinite-order argument uses compatible local covers of an arrangement, so it does not require a faithful linear representation of that group.

A closely related line of inquiry appears in Bogomolov and Katzarkov’s constructions of projective surfaces and their potential counterexamples to the Shafarevich conjecture (Bogomolov and Katzarkov 1998, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BogomolovKatzarkov1998](#ref-BogomolovKatzarkov1998)]. Their proposed obstruction uses infinite chains of compact curves and depends on group-infiniteness statements left conjectural there. Here the obstruction is a rank-three lattice cover of a simple abelian surface. The group-theoretic task is to prove that its cyclic image survives all global relations; the local path criterion supplies that infiniteness argument.

Several established tools make the construction possible. Arithmetic ball quotients supply arbitrarily large regions with exactly prescribed finite hyperplane configurations. Stover and Toledo’s virtual ramified-cover theorem supplies the required sign covers (Stover and Toledo 2022; Llosa Isenrich and Py 2025) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[StoverToledo](#ref-StoverToledo), [LlosaPy](#ref-LlosaPy)], and Zarhin’s theorem supplies a genus-two curve with simple Jacobian (Zarhin 2000) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Zarhin](#ref-Zarhin)]. Standard hyperbolic geometry (Bridson and Haefliger 1999) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger](#ref-BridsonHaefliger)] underlies the local path criterion. Hamm–Lê’s quasi-projective Lefschetz theorem (Hamm and Lê Dũng Tráng 1985) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[HammLe](#ref-HammLe)], together with a deformation argument, allows us to retain a prescribed surface while passing from an orbifold to a smooth projective ambient variety. Each use is stated with its hypotheses at the relevant point below.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The central construction

The principal task is to embed a simple abelian surface in a smooth projective variety so that its fundamental group has infinite cyclic image. Once such an embedding is available, an ample complete intersection in a product with a sufficiently large abelian variety gives Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The complete intersection contains the prescribed surface, and its other fibers over the added abelian variety are finite. This separates the two reasons that a subvariety has infinite fundamental-group image: it either moves in the added abelian variety or lies in the simple surface. Section <a href="#sec:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reduction">2</a> proves this reduction first.

To obtain the cyclic image, we begin with a genus-two curve $C$ whose Jacobian is simple. Its presentation as a double cover of $\mathbb P^1$ uses four branch points in one small disk and two outside that disk. We call the corresponding meridians red and blue. A complex hyperbolic arrangement realizes this marked line as an exceptional fiber. Extra hyperplanes meeting the red part of the pencil impose relations that identify all four red meridians. The sphere relation then identifies the two blue meridians. The image of the genus-two surface group is therefore cyclic: it lies in the cyclic subgroup generated by the product of the resulting red and blue involutions.

These relations alone give only an upper bound. The central difficulty is to prove that the cyclic image is infinite after all global identifications and fillings. We use covers defined separately on large local models of the arrangement. We model their monodromies using the infinite dihedral group generated by two involutions $r,b$; its even words $(rb)^n$ carry the integer translation label $n$. These local monodromies need not define a representation of the full projective fundamental group. Instead, they agree on the endpoint tests needed to read short paths in overlapping charts. The graphs of these local covers are uniformly Gromov hyperbolic. A local path criterion then prevents a loop detected by one chart from becoming trivial through global relations.

The edges of these graphs are paths with bounded *projected diameter*, rather than bounded length. Consequently every power of a fixed loop near the distinguished fiber is a single edge. The local criterion detects every nonzero power separately and gives the infinite-order lower bound. This feature is essential to the argument; neither a global coloring nor a family of finite quotients is needed for that lower bound.

Two copies of the pencil give a distinguished fiber $C\times C$. An order-four symmetry exchanges the factors and rotates their normal directions. Resolving its coarse quotient over this fiber produces a map from a point blowup of $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2 C$, itself the blowup of $\mathop{\mathrm{Jac}}(C)$ at a point. The same local argument detects the sum of the two translation labels. We then realize the resulting surface map in an ordinary smooth projective ambient variety and remove its point blowups by projective modifications. Throughout these operations we preserve the actual homomorphism from the surface group.

The local path criterion and the ambient blowdown construction are formulated separately from the example. The former converts bounded chart compatibility and hyperbolicity into nontriviality of loops. The latter realizes a point blowdown of an embedded surface inside a new smooth projective ambient variety, preserving the ambient fundamental group and the induced surface homomorphism. These two statements isolate the group-theoretic and projective-geometric mechanisms of the construction.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Structure of the proof

Section <a href="#sec:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:paths">3</a> proves the local path criterion independently of the geometric application. Its hypotheses are bounded-range path readings, compatibility of endpoint equality, and uniform hyperbolicity of the reading graphs; no local compactness is required. Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">4</a> constructs the arithmetic arrangement, its sign covers, and the distinguished surface map. Section <a href="#sec:models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:models">5</a> defines the local monodromy labels, proves their buffered compatibility, and establishes hyperbolicity of the associated graphs. Section <a href="#sec:image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:image">6</a> verifies the path criterion for the constructed orbifold and proves that the surface image is infinite cyclic. Section <a href="#sec:projective" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:projective">7</a> carries this image into an ordinary smooth projective variety, removes the point blowups, and completes the proof using Section <a href="#sec:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reduction">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Conventions

All varieties are over $\mathbb C$ unless a field is specified, and all fundamental groups are taken in the usual topology. For a smooth complex orbifold, equivalently a smooth Deligne–Mumford stack in the constructions below, the fundamental group means that of its classifying space. An *ordinary* point has trivial stabilizer. Fundamental-group image statements are understood up to a common choice of basepoints and connecting paths. Projective bundles parametrize lines. Complex hyperbolic distance is normalized so that a point of Euclidean radius $\tanh r$ in the unit ball has distance $r$ from its center.

<a id="sec:reduction"></a>
### Reduction to an abelian surface with cyclic image

The main construction will produce a simple abelian surface inside a smooth projective variety, with infinite cyclic image on fundamental groups. We first explain why that is enough. This separates the geometric obstruction to Steinness from the group-theoretic construction that occupies the intervening sections.

<a id="stmt-prop-large-reduction"></a>
<div id="prop:large-reduction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 2**. *Let $A$ be a simple abelian surface and let $A\hookrightarrow S'$ be a closed embedding in a smooth connected projective complex variety. Suppose that $$\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(\pi_1(A)\longrightarrow\pi_1(S')\bigr)\simeq\mathbb Z.$$ There exist a smooth connected projective fourfold $X$ with large fundamental group and a closed embedding $A\hookrightarrow X$ with infinite cyclic image on fundamental groups, such that the universal cover of $X$ is not Stein.*

</div>

We begin with the equivalence between largeness and the condition on the universal cover used in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="stmt-red-compact-criterion"></a>
<div id="red:compact-criterion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *Let $X$ be a smooth connected projective complex variety. Its universal cover contains no positive-dimensional compact complex-analytic subvariety if and only if $X$ has large fundamental group.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-compact-criterion"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-compact-criterion</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose that $Z\subset X$ has positive dimension and that $\pi_1(Z^\nu)$ has finite image in $\pi_1(X)$. The covering of $Z^\nu$ corresponding to the kernel has finite degree and is compact. Its map to $X$ lifts holomorphically to $\widetilde X$. The image of the lift is a positive-dimensional compact analytic subvariety, by the proper mapping theorem.

Conversely, let $K\subset\widetilde X$ be a positive-dimensional irreducible compact analytic subvariety. Its image $Z$ in $X$ is analytic by the same theorem and algebraic by Chow’s theorem. The restriction $K\to Z$ is finite: it is proper and its fibers are discrete, since the covering map is locally biholomorphic. Thus $K^\nu\to Z^\nu$ is finite and surjective. Such a map has finite-index image on fundamental groups. To see the latter assertion, remove a proper analytic subset of the target so that the map becomes a finite unramified cover of smooth connected spaces. On these open sets the index is its covering degree. Their fundamental groups surject onto those of the normal spaces, by general position for loops; passing to these quotients can only decrease the index. But the composite $\pi_1(K^\nu)\to\pi_1(X)$ is zero, because $K^\nu$ maps to $\widetilde X$. Hence $\pi_1(Z^\nu)$ has finite image in $\pi_1(X)$. ◻

</details>

The next elementary interpolation observation allows us to control all fibers of a general complete intersection simultaneously.

<a id="stmt-red-interpolation"></a>
<div id="red:interpolation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *Let $W$ be a projective variety, $F\subset W$ a closed subscheme, and $L$ a very ample line bundle. Fix a positive integer $\ell$. For all sufficiently large $m$ and every set of distinct points $p_1,\ldots,p_\ell\in W\setminus F$, the evaluation map $$H^0(W,\mathcal I_F\otimes L^m)
 \longrightarrow \bigoplus_{i=1}^{\ell}L^m|_{p_i}$$ is surjective. The same bound on $m$ works for every such set.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-interpolation"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-interpolation</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $m_0$ so that $\mathcal I_F\otimes L^{m_0}$ is globally generated. For each $i$, choose a section $a_i$ nonzero at $p_i$. For every $j\ne i$, very ampleness gives a section of $L$ vanishing at $p_j$ and nonzero at $p_i$. Their product $b_i$ vanishes at all the $p_j$ with $j\ne i$ and is nonzero at $p_i$. The sections $a_ib_i$ therefore give a diagonal basis for evaluation in degree $m_0+\ell-1$. Multiplying each by a section of the remaining power of $L$ nonzero at $p_i$ gives the assertion in every higher degree. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-interpolation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-interpolation</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:large-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:large-reduction">2</a>.* Put $d=\dim S'$, choose an abelian variety $B$ of dimension $g=6$, and write $$W=S'\times B,\qquad F=A\times\{0\},\qquad
 c=d+g-4=d+2.$$ Fix a very ample line bundle $L$ on $W$. We shall take $X$ to be the common zero locus of $c$ general sections of $\mathcal I_F\otimes L^m$, for a sufficiently large $m$. There are two requirements: $X$ must be smooth with the expected fundamental group, and its fibers over $B$ must be finite away from $F$.

<a id="red:fundamental-group"></a>
*Smoothness and the fundamental group.* For large $m$, the sections generate the ideal away from $F$ and their normal derivatives generate $N^*_{F/W}\otimes L^m|_F$. Bertini’s theorem gives smoothness away from $F$. Along $F$, smoothness is the independence of $c$ general vectors in a bundle of rank $d+g-2=c+2$. The rank-deficient matrices have codimension $3$, larger than $\dim F=2$, so no rank loss occurs for a general tuple. The same count for each prefix of the tuple shows that the successive intersections are smooth. They are ample hypersurfaces in their predecessors, with final dimension four. The Lefschetz hyperplane theorem consequently gives connectedness and an isomorphism, induced by inclusion, $$\label{red:fundamental-group}
 \pi_1(X)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }
 \pi_1(S'\times B)=\pi_1(S')\times\pi_1(B).$$ Here and below we use sufficiently high powers of a very ample line bundle to obtain both ideal generation and generation of the indicated normal derivatives; these are the usual applications of Serre vanishing and Bertini’s theorem (Hartshorne 1977) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Hartshorne](#ref-Hartshorne)].

*Fibers away from the fixed surface.* We claim that a general such tuple excludes four distinct points of $W\setminus F$ in any one fiber of $W\to B$. Excluding four points is enough to exclude every positive-dimensional fiber component outside $F$; the choice $g=6$ makes the following incidence count strict. The parameter space of ordered quadruples in one fiber has dimension at most $4d+g=4d+6$. By Lemma <a href="#red:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:interpolation">4</a>, requiring all $c$ equations to vanish at such a quadruple imposes exactly $4c=4d+8$ independent linear conditions on the space of tuples of sections. The incidence variety of tuples and quadruples thus has dimension strictly less than the section parameter space. Its constructible image has closure of strictly smaller dimension as well. A general tuple lies outside this closure. This condition is compatible with the open smoothness conditions already imposed.

It follows that each fiber of $X\to B$ contains at most three points outside $F$. In particular, every positive-dimensional subvariety of a fiber is contained in $F$.

*Largeness.* Let $Z\subset X$ be a positive-dimensional closed irreducible subvariety. If its projection to $B$ is nonconstant, then $\pi_1(Z^\nu)\to\pi_1(B)$ has infinite image. Otherwise its image, a finite subgroup of the torsion-free group $\pi_1(B)$, would be trivial. The map $Z^\nu\to B$ would then lift holomorphically to $\mathbb C^g$. Every holomorphic function on the connected compact normal space $Z^\nu$ is constant, a contradiction.

If the projection is constant, the fiber property gives $Z\subset F$. For $Z=F$, its image in $\pi_1(X)$ is infinite cyclic by <a href="#red:fundamental-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:fundamental-group">[red:fundamental-group]</a> and the hypothesis. The only other possibility is an irreducible curve in $A$. Let $D$ be its smooth normalization. After a translation in $A$, the nonconstant map $D\to A$ extends to a homomorphism $\mathop{\mathrm{Jac}}(D)\to A$. Its image is a nonzero abelian subvariety, and therefore all of $A$ by simplicity. A surjective homomorphism of complex tori carries the source integral lattice to a finite-index subgroup of the target lattice. Consequently $\pi_1(D)$ has finite-index image in $\pi_1(A)\simeq\mathbb Z^4$, and its image under the homomorphism onto the infinite cyclic group remains infinite. This proves largeness in every case. Lemma <a href="#red:compact-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:compact-criterion">3</a> then gives the required absence of compact analytic subvarieties in $\widetilde X$.

*The obstruction to Steinness.* Let $K$ be the kernel of $\pi_1(A)\to\pi_1(X)$. Its quotient is infinite cyclic, so $K\simeq\mathbb Z^3$. A connected component of the inverse image of $F$ in $\widetilde X$ is the connected covering $$A_K=\mathbb C^2/K.$$ It is a closed complex submanifold of $\widetilde X$: the full inverse image of $F$ is closed, and its components are closed and locally separated in covering charts. The real span of $K$ has dimension three, so, as a real manifold, $$A_K\simeq(\mathbb R^3/\mathbb Z^3)\times\mathbb R,
 \qquad H_3(A_K,\mathbb Z)\simeq\mathbb Z.$$ By the Andreotti–Frankel theorem (Andreotti and Frankel 1959) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[AndreottiFrankel1959](#ref-AndreottiFrankel1959)], a Stein manifold of complex dimension two has the homotopy type of a CW complex of real dimension at most two; see (Milnor 1963, Theorem 7.2) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[MilnorMorse](#ref-MilnorMorse)]. Thus $A_K$ is not Stein. A closed complex submanifold of a Stein manifold is Stein, so $\widetilde X$ cannot be Stein either. ◻

</details>

Proposition <a href="#prop:large-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:large-reduction">2</a> leaves one concrete task: embed a simple abelian surface in a smooth projective variety so that its fundamental group has infinite cyclic image. We first construct the corresponding map from a point blowup of the surface to a smooth orbifold, and then convert it to the required embedding in Section <a href="#sec:projective" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:projective">7</a>.

<a id="sec:paths"></a>
### A local criterion for nontrivial loops

The geometric construction will attach group labels to paths only within bounded regions. We therefore need a criterion that detects a nontrivial loop using such local information. The criterion below uses hyperbolic graphs as the local models. It does not require a global map to a model, local finiteness of a graph, or a bound on the information carried by one edge.

A model to keep in mind is a graph whose vertices are points of a space and whose edges are paths confined to small regions. A reading lifts such paths to a covering space chosen near the initial vertex; its endpoint records both the actual endpoint and the sheet of the cover. The argument follows the coarse local-to-global viewpoint of hyperbolic geometry (Gromov 1987) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Gromov1987](#ref-Gromov1987)]; we prove here the version for bounded path readings in compatible charts.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Paths and their readings

All graphs have oriented edges with specified reverses; multiple edges and loops are allowed. Each edge has length one. Paths are finite edge paths, and their lengths count edges. A constant path has length zero. The path groupoid of a graph is obtained by cancelling consecutive reverse edges. We use the path metric on each connected component of a graph.

Fix a positive integer $N$. At each vertex $v$ of a graph $G$, suppose that we are given a pointed graph $(D_v,d_v)$ and a rule for reading every path of length at most $N$ starting at $v$ as an edge path in $D_v$ starting at $d_v$. Write $[p]_v$ for the terminal vertex of the reading of $p$. The following are the required properties.

1.  Readings respect prefixes and reverses of edges, and read a backtrack as a backtrack. If $p$ starts at $v$ and $|p|<N$, reading gives a bijection between the edges leaving the actual endpoint of $p$ and the edges leaving $[p]_v$. If $[p]_v=[p']_v$, then $p$ and $p'$ have the same actual endpoint in $G$, and their edge bijections agree whenever both are defined.

<a id="path:change-root"></a>
2.  Suppose that $p$ runs from $v$ to $w$ and that $a,b$ start at $w$. If each of $p,a,b$ has length at most $N/2$, then $$\label{path:change-root}
     [pa]_v=[pb]_v\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad [a]_w=[b]_w.$$

3.  All connected components of all the graphs $D_v$ are Gromov hyperbolic with a common constant.

These rules concern paths within the stated range. They do not assert that the whole graph $G$ maps to $D_v$, or that the whole graph $D_v$ maps to $G$.

A loop $p$ at $v$ *closes on reading* if $[p]_v=d_v$. Let $\mathcal Q$ be the quotient of the path groupoid of $G$ by the relations that every such loop of length at most three is the constant path.

<a id="stmt-thm-path-lemma"></a>
<div id="thm:path-lemma" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Local path criterion). *There is a bound $N_0$, depending only on the common hyperbolicity constant, with the following property. If the readings satisfy <span class="nodecor">(P1)–(P3)</span> with $N\ge N_0$, and $e$ is a single-edge loop at $v$ satisfying $[e]_v\ne d_v$, then $e$ is nontrivial in $\mathcal Q$.*

</div>

The proof constructs a locally geodesic representative for each path, unique up to a uniformly narrow comparison. Such representatives can be continued one edge at a time. The comparison class is unchanged by the relations defining $\mathcal Q$, but distinguishes the edge in the lemma from the constant path.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Calculations within one chart

We first record precisely how the reading rules allow short calculations to move between charts. By (P1), any edge path in $D_v$ starting at a state already reached by reading can be lifted to a path in $G$, as long as the total reading length is at most $N$. If two such readings have the same endpoint, their lifted paths have the same actual endpoint, and any further common continuation reads identically. Together with (P2), this has three useful consequences.

First, a short circuit closes on reading if and only if its two complementary paths from one corner to another have equal read endpoints. Indeed one may read one path and the reverse of the other, using (P1). Closure can be tested at any corner: a cyclic change of starting corner amounts to prefixing by a side, applying (P2), and cancelling that side using (P1). Here and below, “short” means that all prefixes and continuations in the comparison lie within the ranges of (P1) and (P2).

Second, geodesicity of a short reading is independent of a short prefix. For example, if the reading of $a$ from $w$ is geodesic but its reading after $p:v\to w$ has a shorter competitor, lift that competitor from $[p]_v$. It gives a path $b$ from $w$ with $[pa]_v=[pb]_v$. Equation <a href="#path:change-root" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:change-root">[path:change-root]</a> gives $[a]_w=[b]_w$, contradicting geodesicity. The reverse implication uses a shorter competitor in $D_w$ and the same argument. For distance comparison, suppose the states are reached by paths $a,b$ from $w$. Lift a shortest connector $c$ between their read states, starting after $a$ (or after $pa$). Its length is at most $|a|+|b|$, and apply (P2) to $ac$ and $b$. Doing this in both charts proves equality of the distances whenever $|p|\le N/2$ and $2|a|+|b|\le N/2$. These bounds, as well as their versions with $a,b$ interchanged, hold in every use below.

<a id="path:strip-calculation"></a>
Third, a short strip of closed circuits can be calculated in one chart without adding the lengths of all its transverse paths. Here is the explicit bookkeeping. Let $a_j,b_j$ be the prefixes down the two sides of the strip, and let $\rho_j$ be its transverse path from the endpoint of $a_j$ to that of $b_j$. Starting in the chart at the beginning of the first side, the equalities to prove are $$\label{path:strip-calculation}
 [a_j\rho_j]=[\rho_0b_j].$$ Closure of the next elementary circuit, transported after $a_j$ by (P2), compares its two routes across the next step. Explicitly, write $a_{j+1}=a_js_j$ and $b_{j+1}=b_jt_j$, where $s_j,t_j$ are the next side portions. Closure and then the preceding equality give $$\begin{aligned}
 [a_{j+1}\rho_{j+1}]&=[a_j\rho_jt_j]\\
                    &=[\rho_0b_jt_j]=[\rho_0b_{j+1}],
 \end{aligned}$$ using (P1) for the common continuation, edge by edge. This proves <a href="#path:strip-calculation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:strip-calculation">[path:strip-calculation]</a> at the next step. Thus only the side prefixes and each individual transverse path enter the range bound. Conversely, transverse paths found in this one chart lift to $G$ and give circuits that close when read from their own corners. These facts will justify all uses of diagrams below.

<a id="path:product"></a>
Choose an integer $\Delta\ge10$ large enough for the following standard consequences of the common hyperbolicity bound. In every component of every chart, geodesic triangles are $\Delta$-slim on vertices: every vertex on one side lies within $\Delta$ of a vertex on one of the other two sides. Dividing a geodesic quadrangle by a diagonal then shows that each side lies within $2\Delta$ of the union of the other three sides. The Gromov product $$(x\mid y)_u=\tfrac12\bigl(d(u,x)+d(u,y)-d(x,y)\bigr)$$ satisfies $$\label{path:product}
 (x\mid z)_u\ge
 \min\{(x\mid y)_u,(y\mid z)_u\}-\Delta.$$ Moreover, for a geodesic segment $[x,z]$, there is a vertex on that segment at distance at most $(x\mid z)_u+10\Delta$ from $u$. Enlarging $\Delta$ absorbs all rounding to vertices. These are the usual equivalent forms of hyperbolicity; see (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, vol. 319, chap. III.H) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger](#ref-BridsonHaefliger)].

<a id="path:geodesic-comparison"></a>
We will also use the following elementary comparison. If two geodesic segments $\gamma_1,\gamma_2$, of lengths $\ell_1,\ell_2$, have corresponding endpoints at distance at most $h$, then for every integer $t\ge0$, $$\label{path:geodesic-comparison}
 d\bigl(\gamma_1(\min\{t,\ell_1\}),
        \gamma_2(\min\{t,\ell_2\})\bigr)\le10(h+\Delta).$$ This is a *synchronous comparison* of width $10(h+\Delta)$: the parameters advance by one on both segments until the shorter segment ends, and then that segment waits at its endpoint. To check the bound, join the corresponding endpoints by geodesics of length at most $h$. The resulting quadrangle is $2\Delta$-slim. A point close to the opposite geodesic has a mate whose distance from that geodesic’s initial endpoint differs from its own parameter by at most $h+2\Delta$. A point close to an end connector is within $h+2\Delta$ of the corresponding end. These estimates, together with the bound $2h$ on the difference of the lengths, imply <a href="#path:geodesic-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:geodesic-comparison">[path:geodesic-comparison]</a>, also when one segment has ended.

Fix integers <a id="path:constants"></a>$$\label{path:constants}
 H=1000\Delta,\qquad L\ge10^6H,\qquad k=20L,
 \qquad N\ge10^5k.$$ Taking $L=10^6H$, for example, gives a threshold $N_0=10^5(20L)$ depending only on the original hyperbolicity constant. A *guide* is a path each of whose subpaths of length at most $k$ reads as a geodesic in the chart at its initial vertex. Its entire length may be arbitrarily large. Every individual chart calculation below uses paths, including prefixes, of length less than $1000k$. Consequently (P1) and (P2) always apply. A long guide is handled in separate short pieces, never by reading its whole prefix.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Narrowing comparisons between guides

For paths $\alpha,\beta$ with the same initial and terminal vertices, a *ladder of width $h$* consists of the following data. A finite schedule of pairs of positions begins at $(0,0)$ and ends at $(|\alpha|,|\beta|)$; at each step each position advances by zero or one. At each scheduled pair there is a path, called a rung, from the vertex on $\alpha$ to the vertex on $\beta$, of length at most $h$. The first and last rungs are empty. Each elementary circuit, formed by consecutive rungs and the intervening portions of the two paths, must close on reading. Pausing both paths is allowed. In particular, this definition does not require an elementary circuit to have length at most three. Ladders are comparisons of readings, rather than expressions in the relations defining $\mathcal Q$.

For the widths used below, ladders are symmetric, by changing corners of their elementary circuits. They compose with addition of widths whenever the sum is at most $1000H$, which suffices for every composition below. Indeed, given ladders from $\alpha$ to $\beta$ and from $\beta$ to $\gamma$, synchronize the advances along $\beta$, inserting pauses in the other sides as necessary, and concatenate the two rungs. Each resulting circuit is a union of one or two elementary circuits from the given ladders, so closes by <a href="#path:strip-calculation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:strip-calculation">[path:strip-calculation]</a>. Appending the same trailing path to both sides preserves the width: use empty rungs along that trailing path.

<a id="stmt-path-narrowing"></a>
<div id="path:narrowing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Narrowing). *If two guides have a ladder of width at most $1000H$, then they have one of width at most $H$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-path-narrowing"><summary>Proof of stmt-path-narrowing</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\alpha,\beta$ be the guides, and let the given width be $h\le1000H$. Consider an interval of the schedule on which $\alpha$ advances by at most $8L$. On this interval $\beta$ advances by less than $k$. Otherwise stop at its first advance of $k$ edges. The strip up to that point calculates in one chart: its sides have lengths at most $8L$ and $k$, and its rungs have length at most $h$. The second side is geodesic there, whereas its endpoints can be joined using the first side and the two end rungs. This gives the contradiction $$k\le8L+2h<20L=k.$$ It follows also that on every such schedule interval both sides read as geodesics, and their lengths differ by at most $2h$.

Suppose first that $|\alpha|\ge L$. Mark $\alpha$ at $x_0,\ldots,x_m$, including its endpoints, so that consecutive marked intervals have lengths in $[L,2L]$. Choose scheduled mates $b_0,\ldots,b_m$ on $\beta$, using its actual endpoints for $b_0,b_m$. The distance along $\beta$ between consecutive old mates is at least $L-2h$.

<figure id="fig:path-ladder">

<figcaption>Narrowing a ladder in a single chart. Both displayed guide pieces read as geodesics. The interior mark $x_{i}$ is far from the end rungs, so quadrangle slimness supplies a new mate $b_{i}′$ on the opposite side, within $2Δ$ of $x_{i}$. The old scheduled mate $b_{i}$ is at distance at most $h + 2Δ$ from $b_{i}′$ along that side. The drawing is schematic: the long side intervals and the two rung bounds use different visual scales.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-path-ladder"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:path-ladder`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Latex,
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  guide/.style={line width=.9pt},
  oldrung/.style={densely dashed,line width=.65pt,black!65},
  newrung/.style={line width=1.1pt,ladderaccent}]
  \coordinate (am) at (0,2.05);
  \coordinate (ai) at (5.4,2.05);
  \coordinate (ap) at (10.8,2.05);
  \coordinate (bm) at (0,.2);
  \coordinate (bo) at (6.7,.2);
  \coordinate (bn) at (5.4,.2);
  \coordinate (bp) at (10.8,.2);
  \draw[guide] (am)--(ap);
  \draw[guide] (bm)--(bp);
  \draw[oldrung] (am)--node[left] {$\le h$} (bm);
  \draw[oldrung] (ap)--node[right] {$\le h$} (bp);
  \draw[oldrung] (ai)--node[pos=.50,right,fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt]
    {old: $\le h$} (bo);
  \draw[newrung] (ai)--node[pos=.55,left,fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt]
    {new: $\le 2\Delta$} (bn);
  \foreach \p in {am,ai,ap,bm,bo,bp}
    \fill (\p) circle (1.7pt);
  \draw[newrung,fill=white] (bn) circle (2.2pt);
  \node[above=4pt] at (am) {$x_{i-1}$};
  \node[above=4pt] at (ai) {$x_i$};
  \node[above=4pt] at (ap) {$x_{i+1}$};
  \node[below=5pt] at (bm) {$b_{i-1}$};
  \node[below=5pt] at (bn) {$b_i'$};
  \node[below=5pt] at (bo) {$b_i$};
  \node[below=5pt] at (bp) {$b_{i+1}$};

  \node[left=18pt] at (bm) {$\beta$};
  \draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt}]
    (0,2.76)--node[above=5pt] {length in $[L,2L]$} (5.4,2.76);
  \draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt}]
    (5.4,2.76)--node[above=5pt] {length in $[L,2L]$} (10.8,2.76);
```
